LLMTabBench: Evaluating LLMs on Binary Tabular Classification
From Zero to Few Shots
Daria Grushina

Abstract
Supervised classification for tabular data remains a core machine
learning task, yet its reliance on large labeled datasets limits ap-
plicability in data-scarce domains. For such few-shot scenarios,
specialized methods like TabPFN — a state-of-the-art Prior-Data
Fitted Network — have set a high standard by leveraging large-
scale synthetic pretraining, though they still require a context of
labeled examples to function. In contrast, Large Language Models
(LLMs) could offer a more flexible alternative via zero- and few-
shot in-context learning directly from task descriptions, but their
performance on tabular data remains inconsistent and poorly un-
derstood. We introduce LLMTabBench, a benchmark designed to
systematically evaluate LLMs for tabular classification under data-
scarce conditions. LLMTabBench explicitly probes (i) how LLM
prior knowledge interacts with in-context information (task de-
scriptions and few-shot examples), and (ii) how model performance
scales with increasing data complexity, using both real-world and
controlled synthetic datasets. Our findings include: (1) LLMs are
highly competitive in zero-shot settings and can outperform alter-
native models, even when those models have access to few-shot
examples; (2) incorporating additional few-shot examples can con-
flict with LLM prior knowledge, limiting or even degrading perfor-
mance; and (3) there is a data complexity threshold beyond which
LLMs’ performance declines and few-shot examples become less
effective. Together, these findings reveal fundamental constraints of
in-context learning for tabular data and provide practical guidance
for deploying LLMs in low-data regimes.
Keywords
Tabular Data, Benchmark, Large Language Models, Few-Shot, Zero-
Shot, In-Context
1
Introduction
Tabular classification remains one of the most widespread and
critical tasks across various industries, including healthcare, finance,
and marketing [34]. While classical machine learning (ML) models
like gradient boosting machines [6, 23, 29] and specialized neural
networks [1, 5, 16, 21] excel when large volumes of labeled historical
data are available, they consistently struggle in low-data regimes
and cross-task generalization, i.e., transferring to new datasets or
targets with minimal labeled data.
For the challenging few-shot regime, where only a small number
of labeled examples are available, Prior-Data Fitted Networks (PFNs)
Figure 1: 0-shot LLM vs. 16-shot TabPFN: Average ROC-AUC
on 8 domains
and their tabular-specific variant, TabPFN [20], have set a new stan-
dard. Through large-scale pretraining on synthetic data, TabPFN
achieves remarkable in-context few-shot performance. However, as
a specialized few-shot learner, it fundamentally requires a context
of labeled examples to function and cannot perform inference in
their complete absence.
The emergence of Large Language Models (LLMs) has introduced
a paradigm shift in tabular classification. Traditionally, adapting a
model to a specific task involves full fine-tuning, where the model’s
internal weights are updated using a dataset of labeled examples.
A more efficient variant is few-shot learning, where the model is
updated or adapted using only a small number of samples. These
approaches, while effective [19, 22], typically require substantial
computational resources for weight optimization. In contrast, in-
context learning presents a fundamentally different mechanism: it
does not update the model’s weights. Instead, task instructions
and a few labeled examples (shots) are provided within the input
prompt, and the model performs inference based on this contextual
information alone. This offers a highly resource-efficient alterna-
tive, though its effectiveness can be inconsistent across tasks and
arXiv:2605.24417v1  [cs.LG]  23 May 2026


## Page 2


domains [26, 31]. Critically, all three paradigms—fine-tuning, few-
shot learning, and in-context learning—share the same fundamental
bottleneck as TabPFN: they depend on the availability of a small,
yet representative, labeled sample, which remains a significant lim-
itation in real-world applications. In many practical scenarios, such
data are unavailable, costly to obtain, or entirely nonexistent.
This limitation underscores the need for robust zero-shot tabular
classification, where a model produces accurate predictions without
any task-specific training examples. In this work, we systematically
evaluate the effectiveness of LLMs in zero-shot settings. We demon-
strate that for certain domains and problem structures, zero-shot
LLMs can outperform both few-shot learning and classical ML mod-
els trained on the full training dataset. To investigate the limits of
this capability, we introduce the concept of data complexity to de-
fine the types of problems that LLMs can reliably solve. We quantify
data complexity using a TabPFN-based proxy, enabling a principled
assessment of task difficulty. Furthermore, we illustrate how the
injection of prior and external expert knowledge can compensate
for the shortcomings of the raw zero-shot regime.
Beyond the zero-shot paradigm, we further explore the effective-
ness of few-shot learning in an in-context setting. Crucially, we
investigate how its utility can be significantly enhanced through
augmentation with various forms of knowledge. In particular, we
examine the integration of prior knowledge along with external
knowledge.
Our results confirm that while zero-shot LLMs may not univer-
sally outperform models trained on abundant data, they provide
a powerful and indispensable solution for the "cold-start" prob-
lem, achieving remarkable performance where traditional methods
are entirely inapplicable. Furthermore, the strategic enhancement
of few-shot learning with integrated knowledge creates a robust
framework for tackling tabular classification in even the most chal-
lenging low-data environments. The main contributions of this
work are articulated as follows:
• We introduce LLMTabBench1, a benchmark for zero-shot
and few-shot in-context tabular classification with LLMs in
labeled-scarce settings.
• We investigate knowledge conflicts in zero-shot and few-shot
regimes, examining how LLMs synthesize domain-specific
prior knowledge and in-context information (task descrip-
tions and shots).
• We study how LLMs detect and exploit patterns in tabular
data, and introduce a data complexity concept to quantify
how task difficulty affects LLM predictions.
• We study serialization strategies and provide recommenda-
tions for robust tabular-to-text representations.
• We perform statistical significance testing across tasks to
support reliable model comparisons.
2
Related Works
Existing tabular benchmarks primarily target fully supervised train-
ing. Suites such as OpenML-CC18 [3] and stress-test collections like
TabZilla [27], as well as living benchmarks such as TabArena [9],
standardize evaluation pipelines but do not control prompt design,
1https://github.com/sb-ai-lab/llm4tab
context budgets, or how labels are allocated as demonstrations for
in-context learning.
Benchmarks that explicitly probe LLMs on tabular prediction
are fewer. TABLET [31] highlights instruction and formatting sen-
sitivity but does not separate the effects of task descriptions versus
demonstrations or systematically vary complexity; TabularMath [7]
diagnoses interpolation–extrapolation using program-verified syn-
thetic tables, but its deterministic, narrow setup limits coverage of
real-world tabular classification. The critique in [17] further argues
that reported tabular LLM gains can be inflated by task aggregation,
leakage/contamination, and format familiarity, motivating stricter
controls when assessing generalization.
A related body of work evaluates LLMs on table understanding
and TableQA (FewTUD [25], [33], TableBench [35]), which empha-
sizes structural reasoning but differs from row-wise classification
under scarce labels. LLMTabBench addresses this gap by target-
ing low-data tabular classification while systematically varying
in-context information and dataset complexity across real and con-
trolled synthetic settings.
3
LLMTabBench Overview and Methodology
Motivation
3.1
LLMTabBench at a Glance
LLMTabBench evaluates low-data tabular classification by isolat-
ing serialization, demonstration count, task-knowledge cues, and
dataset complexity.
Dataset suites. LLMTabBench contains 95 datasets organized
into complementary suites (Table 2). The core real-world suite
includes 24 publicly available datasets sourced from OpenML [3]
and Kaggle and grouped into 8 knowledge domains (3 datasets per
domain). All datasets use fixed, class-balanced splits produced by
Repeated Stratified K-Fold (𝑘=3), reusing the first fold’s train/test
partition across all experiments.

Evaluation axes. We evaluate models under a factorial grid span-
ning:
• Shot count. Zero-shot and 𝑘∈{4, 8, 16, 32, 64} in-context
demonstrations.
• Serialization. Five tabular-to-text formats: (i) feat_val,
(ii) feat_val_mask, (iii) markdown, (iv) markdown_mask, (v)
html. The complexity grid uses two masked serializations.

Detailed description of these serializations is available in
Section 4
• Prompt type. Contextual prompting (instruction + demon-
strations) throughout; additionally, the synthetic suite sup-
ports a context-free prompt and a knowledge-augmented
prompt that injects LLM-generated decision rules.
• Demonstration sampling. For each few-shot setting, we
run 5 independent stratified draws of the 𝑘demonstrations
to reduce variance from example choice.
Models and baselines. We benchmark six LLMs: GPT-4o-mini,
Qwen3-1.7B, Qwen3-8B, Qwen3-14B [36]. For open-weight models,
we compare two inference modes: (i) forward scoring (predictions
are obtained from token-level log-probabilities of two target labels
and normalized using a sigmoid function) and (ii) generation (models
produce free-form text outputs that are subsequently mapped to
class labels). We also include five baselines: TabPFN-2.5 [18], TabICL
[30], XGBoost [6], Random Forest, and Logistic Regression.
Serialization schemas.
• feat_val: A simple “Feature = value” listing. Example: "Fea-
tures are: age = 39, education = Bachelor, gain = 2174. Answer
is 0."
• feat_val_mask: A ”Feature = value” listing with columns
masking as proxy names. Example: "Features are: x_1 = 39,
x_2 = Bachelor, x_3 = 2174. Answer is: y = 0."
• markdown: A Markdown-formatted table.
• markdown_mask: A Markdown table with feature masking.
• html: An HTML-formatted table.
Scoring. We report mean ROC-AUC over five demonstration
draws per setting, which reduces sensitivity to example choice.
ROC-AUC avoids threshold tuning and supports forward-scoring
outputs.
3.2
What Questions Can LLMTabBench
Answer?
LLMTabBench is designed not only as a leaderboard for tabular
classification with LLMs, but as a diagnostic benchmark that decom-
poses in-context performance into separable factors. The bench-
mark isolates the effects of (i) tabular-to-text serialization, (ii) the
number and selection of in-context demonstrations, and (iii) task
knowledge information available in the prompt (e.g., column names
and natural-language task descriptions), (iv) potential data contam-
ination from LLM pre-training and (v) intrinsic data complexity.
Research questions. LLMTabBench supports the following bench-
mark questions, each backed by a dedicated suite and/or ablation
protocol:
RQ1 How competitive are LLMs in zero-shot tabular clas-
sification? We evaluate the effectiveness of large language
models for tabular classification when no labeled examples
are available. In particular, we assess whether zero-shot LLM
performance exceeds an adaptive random-guessing baseline.
RQ2 To what extent does zero-shot performance depend on
in-context information versus LLM prior knowledge?
We analyze whether high zero-shot performance arises pri-
marily from models’ prior (pretraining) knowledge or from
in-context information provided at inference time. By pro-
gressively removing in-context information, we examine
how performance degrades and thereby disentangle the rela-
tive contributions of in-context information and prior knowl-
edge.
RQ3 Do LLMs effectively leverage few-shot demonstrations
for tabular classification? We investigate whether large
language models meaningfully utilize few-shot labeled ex-
amples, and whether predictive performance systematically
improves as the number of shots increases.
RQ4 How does removing in-context information affect few-
shot performance across shot regimes? We study how
model performance changes when in-context information
within few-shot prompts is systematically ablated, and whether
the magnitude of this effect depends on the number of pro-
vided shots. This analysis clarifies how strongly few-shot
learning relies on explicit contextual supervision at different
shot counts.
RQ5 Does combining in-context information, few-shot ex-
amples, and external instructions improve performance?
We examine whether jointly providing explicit in-context
information, few-shot demonstrations, and external knowl-
edge in the form of decision rules leads to improved predic-
tive performance. We further assess whether their combined
use yields consistent gains over using each source in isola-
tion.
RQ6 How does dataset complexity influence LLM perfor-
mance on tabular tasks? We study the impact of dataset
complexity on the predictive performance of large language
models. By systematically varying complexity in controlled
synthetic datasets, we identify performance limits as task
difficulty increases.
3


## Page 4


Together, these research questions can use LLMTabBench to
characterize how large language models perform on tabular clas-
sification under a low-data regime. They isolate the roles of prior
knowledge, in-context information, the number of few-shot ex-
amples, and dataset complexity, and examine how these factors
interact. This framework enables a systematic analysis of when
LLMs act as effective tabular classifiers and where their limitations
emerge.
4
LLMTabBench Construction and Evaluation
Protocol
Here we describe each of the settings in the RQs in detail, including
task construction and evaluation protocol.
4.1
RQ1: How competitive are LLMs in
zero-shot tabular classification?
This benchmark evaluates the zero-shot performance of multiple
large language models (LLMs) on a binary classification task.
Experimental Design. We evaluate 4 LLMs, 2 inference modes,
and 5 serialization regimes as mentioned in Section 3. For this RQ
24 binary classification datasets from the core real-world suite are
used (see Table 2).
Statistical Analysis. To determine if model performance is
statistically superior to chance, we apply a simulation-based hy-
pothesis test for each dataset. Specifically, we estimate whether the
LLMs ROC-AUC is significantly above the random baseline (see
Appendix C.1 for details).
Meta-Analytic Aggregation To generalize findings across all
24 datasets, a Fixed-Effects Meta-Analysis (FEM) with Stouffer’s
method (also known as the weighted Z-transform method) [32]
is conducted. The dataset-level p-values are aggregated under a
fixed-effect assumption, namely that the true effect is shared across
datasets.
4.2
RQ2: To what extent does zero-shot
performance depend on in-context
information versus LLM prior knowledge?
This setting assesses whether zero-shot performance is primarily
attributable to pretrained knowledge or to in-context information
by measuring performance degradation under progressive removal
of in-context information.
Experimental design. We use the same fixed grid as above,
except for additional ablations explained below.
• Context Prompt (2 Levels):
– With Context: A prompt containing an explicit task de-
scription specifying the domain, classification objective,
and target labels.
– Without Context: A neutral instruction that does not
disclose the domain, task, or target values (e.g., "Perform
binary classification for the following row.").
• Headers (2 Levels): Semantic: Real columns’ names. Generic:
Placeholders instead of them.
• Graded Context Configuration (4 Levels): combining the
Context Prompt (With Context / Without Context) with the
Headers (Semantic / Generic) types (see Table 3 for descrip-
tion).

To measure the context contribution we progressively reduce
the explicit contextual cues available to the model — replace the se-
mantic names of the columns with generic placeholders and change
the descriptive task prompt to a neutral, context-free alternative.
4.3
RQ3: Do LLMs effectively leverage few-shot
demonstrations for tabular classification?
This setting aims to quantify the performance gains from providing
few-shot examples in a binary classification task and to assess
whether these gains are statistically significant relative to a zero-
shot baseline across datasets and model architectures.
Experimental design. We use the same fixed grid as in RQ1. The
core modification involves the introduction of a few-shot example
into the prompting strategy. For each experimental run with 𝑘∈
{4, 8, 16, 32, 64}, 𝑘correctly labeled examples are randomly sampled
from the training set and prefixed to the test query as an in-context
demonstration. Using the predicted probabilities and the true labels
for all test instances, the overall ROC-AUC score is computed. This
yields, for each experimental cell (Model, Inference Mode, Dataset),
a series of ROC-AUC scores: one for 𝑘= 0 and one for each 𝑘∈
{4, 8, 16, 32, 64}.
Statistical Analysis. To rigorously assess the significance of per-
formance increments from zero-shot to 𝑘-shot learning, a two-step
analytical procedure - Pairwise Model Comparison via DeLong’s
Test and Stouffer’s method of 𝑝-values aggragetion - is employed
(see Appendix C.2 for details).
4.4
RQ4: How does removing in-context
information affect few-shot performance
across shot regimes?
This section examines how few-shot examples interact with explicit
contextual information in binary classification, specifically whether
they enable models to compensate for missing task descriptions or
semantic feature names.
4


## Page 5


Experimental Design. This study extends the design estab-
lished in RQ2 by introducing few-shot examples while maintaining
the same systematic variation of contextual information.
Procedure. For each unique combination of factors (Model ×
Serialization × Context Prompt × Mode × Graded Configuration ×
𝑘× Seed), 𝑘correctly labeled examples are randomly sampled from
the training set. A prompt is then constructed by including either a
context-specific prompt or a neutral instruction, appending the 𝑘
few-shot examples formatted according to the chosen serialization
method, and adding the test query using the same format.
To evaluate whether few-shot examples can compensate for miss-
ing contextual information, we conduct planned contrasts compar-
ing minimal-context conditions with a high number of few-shot
examples (e.g., (𝑘= 64)) to maximal-context conditions with a small
number of examples (e.g., (𝑘= 4)). In addition, we analyze how
performance scales with increasing 𝑘across different contextual
configurations.
4.5
RQ5: Does combining in-context
information, few-shot examples, and
external instructions improve performance?
This setting investigates whether combining explicit in-context
information, few-shot demonstrations, and external knowledge in
the form of decision rules improves predictive performance beyond
using each source in isolation.
Experimental Design. This study extends the design from
RQ4 by introducing two novel experimental dimensions: synthetic
datasets containing model-specific prior knowledge, and explicit
decision rules as external instructions.
The experimental design varies two additional factors. First,
we consider two dataset types: real datasets, consisting of the
24 datasets used in previous RQs, and synthetic datasets, which
are model-generated counterparts created by prompting the same
model to produce synthetic examples reflecting its learned priors
about the domain, task, and label distribution Second, we vary the
type of external instructions provided in the prompt across three
conditions: a baseline condition with no external instructions be-
yond context and few-shot examples; a condition where synthetic
dataset examples generated by the model are included alongside
or in place of real few-shot examples; and a condition where the
prompt includes explicit decision rules generated by the LLM (e.g.,
"If 𝑥1 > 0.5 and 𝑥3 < 2.0, then class = 1") and expressed as if–then
statements.
4.6
RQ6: How does dataset complexity influence
LLM performance on tabular tasks?
We analyze how increasing dataset complexity affects LLM pre-
dictive performance using controlled synthetic datasets.Synthetic
Complexity Grid Generation. To construct a controlled bench-
mark for evaluating pattern recognition capacity, we generate a grid
of synthetic classification datasets using an MLP-based TabPFN-
style [20] prior that enables explicit control over task complexity.
Dataset difficulty is governed by three parameters: network depth
(number of hidden layers), network width (input dimensionality),
and hidden state size. We define seven discrete difficulty levels
with parameter settings summarized in Table 14, and generate five
datasets per level using different random seeds, yielding a total of
35 synthetic datasets.
Experiment Design. We use the same models, inference modes,
and zero-/few-shot settings as in the previous RQs. Data are serial-
ized using two masked formats, which are functionally equivalent
in this context-free setting due to the absence of semantic feature
headers. A single context-free prompt is employed, containing ei-
ther only the formatted query instance in the zero-shot setting or
𝑘demonstration examples followed by the query instance in the
few-shot setting. For each of the 35 synthetic datasets, experiments
are conducted across all relevant combinations of factors.
Statistical Analysis. To assess whether LLMs can learn classifi-
cation rules from data alone without semantic context, we compare
𝑘-shot performance to a zero-shot baseline using the same proce-
dure as in RQ3. The null hypothesis states that 𝑘-shot performance
with pure data patterns does not exceed zero-shot performance
across all datasets.
5
Results
5.1
RQ1: How competitive are LLMs in
zero-shot tabular classification?
Our headline finding is that zero-shot LLM predictions are roughly
on par with the 16-shot performance of strong conventional base-
lines. Aggregated across all 32 datasets (Table 4), Qwen3-14B reaches
a mean ROC–AUC of 0.714 in the zero-shot regime, GPT-4o-mini
0.712, and Qwen3-8B 0.698, against 0.749 for 16-shot TabPFN. The
smallest model we test, Qwen3-1.7B, trails noticeably at 0.626, so
the parity is a property of the mid-sized and larger LLMs rather
than the family as a whole. Within healthcare the picture is even
stronger: zero-shot LLMs surpass every baseline trained on the full
training set, TabPFN included (avg. LLM 0-shot 0.897 vs. 0.761 for
TabPFN-2.5; see Figure 7 in Appendix D.1).
The Student’s 𝑡-test aggregated with Fisher’s exact method con-
firms that zero-shot LLM predictions land significantly above a
Dirichlet-sampled random baseline (Section 4): the difference is
significant on 18 of 24 classic datasets, with the FEM aggregated
𝑝-value = 0 (numerical evidence in Table 4).
A natural worry is that these scores reflect dataset memorization
rather than generalizable reasoning. To probe this we introduced
eight evaluation datasets covering distinct domains, all published
after a conservative knowledge-cutoff of October 2024 (set by GPT-
4o-mini). Because a temporally clean train-test split on the same
underlying data is not feasible, we proxy it by comparing established
benchmarks (“classic”) against newer datasets from similar domains
(“new”).
The drop on temporally newer datasets is consistent but not
catastrophic. Comparing the Classic and New rows of Table 4, the
LLMs lose 0.053–0.076 ROC–AUC, with Qwen3-14B falling the
most. TabPFN itself drops by 0.074, so the degradation is broadly
shared rather than LLM-specific. Under severe data contamination
one would expect the LLMs to collapse on the new datasets while
TabPFN remained stable; this is not what we observe. Contamina-
tion on individual benchmarks cannot be ruled out, but it does not
appear to be the dominant explanation for the overall zero-shot
performance.

established benchmarks), and New (8 datasets published after October 2024).
5.1.1
Direct memorization probes. To complement the aggregate
old/new comparison with per-dataset evidence, we apply a two-step
probe to all 24 classic datasets on Qwen3-14B and GPT-4o-mini.
The first step is a recognition test: given only the dataset name,
the model is asked to describe it and list its exact feature columns
and target. Reproducing idiosyncratic column names (e.g. fnlwgt),
which cannot be guessed from the name alone, indicates exposure to
the actual data. We label each dataset leaked, knows-name-only,
or not-recognized. The second step is a reconstruction test,
applied to every dataset flagged in step 1: we sample 100 rows, mask
50 random cells, and prompt the model to fill each one from the
rest of its row. To separate memorization from generic tabular skill,
we run the same protocol on a column-shuffled copy (each column
independently permuted, preserving marginals but breaking row
identity); the lift of accuracy on real rows over shuffled rows isolates
row-specific knowledge. We treat lift > 0.10 as likely-leaked and
lift > 0.05 as suspicious.
Results. Table 5 reports recognition outcomes: Qwen3-14B rec-
ognizes 11/24 datasets at both steps, GPT-4o-mini only 3/24, while
the latter shows knows-name-only behaviour much more often (9
vs. 2). This pattern is consistent with exposure to dataset documen-
tation but not to the rows themselves.
Reconstruction (Table 6) gives a far more conservative picture.
Only biodegr (Qwen3-14B, lift +0.13) and adult (GPT-4o-mini, lift
+0.16) clear the likely-leaked threshold; tae and compas reach
suspicious; the rest sit at zero or below. Tellingly, adult is flagged

tion.
Combined with the old/new comparison, the probes find strong
memorization on at most one dataset per model, with a handful of
suspicious cases. The high zero-shot performance therefore cannot
be primarily attributed to row-level memorization, although small
per-dataset contamination effects cannot be ruled out.
Answer to RQ1. Mid-sized and larger LLMs are competitive with
strong conventional baselines in zero-shot tabular classification:
Qwen3-14B (0.714) and GPT-4o-mini (0.712) come within 0.04 ROC–
AUC of 16-shot TabPFN (0.749) averaged over all 32 datasets. On the
temporally clean “new” subset every LLM degrades by an amount
comparable to TabPFN’s own drop, and direct probes flag at most
one dataset per model as strongly leaked. The competitive zero-shot

5.2
RQ2: To what extent does zero-shot
performance depend on in-context
information versus LLM prior knowledge?
The strong zero-shot performance reported in RQ1 admits two
possible mechanisms: the model may draw on prior knowledge
acquired during pre-training, or it may infer the task directly from
the in-context information in the prompt. To separate the two we
run a controlled ablation, varying two channels through which
in-context information reaches the model (Section 4): Schema indi-
cators (the column names that appear in the test row and any shot
examples) and Task context (the natural-language description in the
user prompt). Crossing the two yields four 0-shot configurations,
reported in Table 7.
Two patterns stand out. First, both channels carry real signal:
for the three mid-to-large models, moving from NoCols-NoContext
to Cols-Context gains 0.10–0.14 ROC–AUC (GPT-4o-mini 0.625 →
0.728; Qwen3-8B 0.579 →0.711; Qwen3-14B 0.596 →0.733). The
two channels are complementary rather than redundant – the joint
gain exceeds either channel alone – and schema indicators on their
own already account for most of it.
Second, there is a clear scaling effect (Figure 10 in Appendix D.2):
the benefit of in-context information grows with model size for
models above 8 B parameters. Qwen3-1.7B is the exception. It im-
proves with columns (0.606 →0.653) but actually degrades once
the task description is added on top (0.653 →0.640 in the Cols-
Context configuration). The small model appears unable to exploit
the natural-language description and is hurt by the extra input,
while the larger models extract additional structure from it.
Answer to RQ2. In-context information is the dominant driver of
zero-shot performance for the mid-to-large LLMs, accounting for
0.10–0.14 ROC–AUC over a baseline with neither column names
nor task description. Schema indicators alone produce most of
the gain, and the natural-language task description adds a further
boost only at the 8 B parameter scale and above; below that, prior
knowledge from pre-training appears to suffice on its own and the
task description becomes counterproductive.
5.3
RQ3: Do LLMs effectively leverage few-shot
demonstrations for tabular classification?
Although zero-shot LLM inference reaches the same range as 16-
shot TabPFN, it does not consistently surpass it, which suggests that
in-context information alone is not enough to clearly beat strong
baselines. This motivates a closer look at how LLMs use few-shot
examples.
Behaviour is heterogeneous across domains: in healthcare and
education adding more shots yields no measurable ROC–AUC im-
provement, while in natural science, law, and people-and-society
performance scales positively with the number of examples (per-
domain breakdowns are in Appendix E). To put this on a statistical
footing we run DeLong’s test on the per-dataset results aggregated
across the real-world suite via Stouffer’s method. Every few-shot
configuration produces a significant improvement over the zero-
shot baseline across the Qwen3 family (Table 8), with the sole
exception of the 4-shot configuration of Qwen3-14B. LLMs do use
in-context examples.
Table 8 gives the aggregated trajectory on the classic suite under
the Cols-Context prompt. The improvements are real but flatten
quickly: GPT-4o-mini gains 0.728 →0.793 (+0.065), Qwen3-8B
0.711 →0.790 (+0.079), Qwen3-14B 0.733 →0.801 (+0.068), and
the bulk of each gain is already realised by 16 shots (0.774 / 0.778 /
0.784 respectively). Beyond 16 shots the returns diminish sharply, so
aggregating more than 16 demonstrations buys little. Qwen3-1.7B
follows the same shape but at a lower level (0.640 →0.715).
Finally, the marginal benefit of few-shot examples decreases with
model size (Figure 12 in Appendix D.3). Larger models start from

the patterns that smaller models still have to learn from explicit

Answer to RQ3. LLMs do leverage few-shot demonstrations, with
statistically significant gains over the zero-shot baseline across
nearly every configuration of the Qwen3 family. The gains are mod-
est in absolute terms (+0.065 to +0.079 ROC–AUC at 64 shots over
0-shot) and saturate near 16 shots, so adding more examples beyond
that yields diminishing returns. The effect is also size-dependent:
larger models benefit less, consistent with a greater reliance on
prior knowledge than on in-context demonstrations.
5.4
RQ4: How does removing in-context
information affect few-shot performance
across shot regimes?
The previous results hint at a trade-off between in-context infor-
mation and the number of few-shot examples: models that benefit
strongly from one tend to benefit less from the other. Here we
make that relationship explicit by tracking how the contribution
of in-context information changes as the shot count grows, and
whether the same pattern holds across model sizes.
Figure 3 and Table 9 show that the interaction is non-additive.
Each cell in the table is the ROC–AUC gap between the richest
prompt (Cols-Context) and the poorest one (NoCols-NoContext) at
the same shot count. The gap shrinks monotonically with 𝑘for
every model that uses in-context information at all: GPT-4o-mini
falls from +0.103 at 𝑘= 0 to +0.032 at 𝑘= 64; Qwen3-8B falls from
+0.132 to +0.023; Qwen3-14B from +0.137 to +0.026. Qwen3-1.7B is
again the exception – the gap is small at 𝑘= 0 (+0.034) and decays
to essentially zero (+0.001) by 𝑘= 64, consistent with its weak use
of context across the board.
The natural reading is that in-context information and few-shot
examples partly substitute for each other rather than adding up.
When few examples are available the model leans on its prior knowl-
edge to get started; as more demonstrations come in, the model
leans on them instead, and the relative importance of pre-existing
knowledge drops. Prior knowledge mainly helps bootstrap perfor-
mance when data are scarce.
A second, more subtle effect emerges in the high-data regime.
Once enough in-context examples are available, the complete ab-
sence of contextual cues (NoCols-NoContext) can outperform con-
figurations with partial cues (NoCols-Context, Cols-NoContext). The
effect is clearest for GPT-4o-mini in Figure 3, where the no-context,
no-schema setting overtakes its intermediate counterparts at higher
shot counts. This points to an interference effect: when prior cues
and few-shot examples are both partially present, they can conflict
rather than reinforce. Our working hypothesis is that prompt-level
cues (such as suggestive column names) bias the model towards its
pretraining distribution, while the in-context examples provide a
competing task-specific signal, and the partial-cue configurations
sit in the worst-of-both-worlds middle.
Answer to RQ4. Removing in-context information hurts the most
at 𝑘= 0 and progressively less as the shot count grows: the Cols-
Context vs. NoCols-NoContext gap drops from ∼+0.10–+0.14 ROC–
AUC at 0-shot to ∼+0.02–+0.03 at 64-shot for the mid-to-large
models. In-context information and few-shot demonstrations there-
fore act as partial substitutes rather than as additive resources, with
prior knowledge mainly bootstrapping performance in data-scarce
regimes. At high shot counts, partial cues can even hurt – consistent
with an interference effect between prompt-level priors and the
task-specific signal carried by the demonstrations.
5.5
RQ5: Does combining in-context
information, few-shot examples, and
external instructions improve performance?
Having established the central role of in-context information for
LLM tabular classification, we now ask whether performance de-
pends on the alignment between the contextual information in the
prompt and the model’s parametric knowledge. To control that
alignment we use the LLM-synthetic suite (see Table 2): 24 datasets

value
each generated by an LLM to mirror a real-world counterpart, to-
gether with the decision rules the same LLM produced during gen-
eration. The setup lets us evaluate combinations of three in-context
learning entities – few-shot demonstrations, the natural language
task description (prior), and the LLM-distilled decision rules (expert)
– on data whose structure the model already understands.
Two surface-level observations from Figures 20 and 21 (Appen-
dix D.5) are worth flagging before we look at the combinations.
First, for half of the models reduced-context settings outperform
full-context configurations. Second, intermediate context levels of-
ten work best. A plausible explanation is that with less context
the model leans more heavily on its parametric knowledge, and
on generator-aligned data that knowledge is already well-matched
to the task. Importantly, adding contextual information does not
degrade performance relative to real-data baselines: there is no
obvious trade-off between context and few-shot examples on this
suite.
Table 10 reports the seven combinations against the TabPFN-16
baseline. We highlight three patterns.
Single regimes. No single regime dominates: which entity wins
depends on the model. Prior alone is best for Qwen3-8B (0.667) and
GPT-4o-mini (0.734); Few-shot (16) alone is best for Qwen3-1.7B
(0.620) and Qwen3-14B (0.695). Expert alone is never the best single
choice. When only one channel is available, the trade-off between
Few-shot (16) and Prior therefore appears to be roughly even.
Pairwise combinations. Combining two entities almost always
helps. The clearest win is Few-shot + prior, which improves over ei-
ther component for every model (e.g. GPT-4o-mini 0.795 vs. 0.734/0.718;
Qwen3-8B 0.772 vs. 0.667/0.654). The exception is Few-shot + expert
on Qwen3-1.7B, which actually drops below Few-shot alone (0.590
vs. 0.620): the smallest model cannot apply its own distilled rules
on top of demonstrations, even though it generated those rules
itself. The mid-to-large models handle the same combination fine,
suggesting that successfully using expert rules requires a minimum
capacity.
Best overall. The strongest results come from combining all three
(Few-shot + expert + prior) or pairing Expert + prior, depending on
the model. Expert + prior (0-shot) is the top configuration for Qwen3-
1.7B (0.732) and Qwen3-14B (0.862), while the full triple is best for
Qwen3-8B (0.829) and GPT-4o-mini (0.818). All best configurations
clear the TabPFN-16 baseline of ∼0.72, sometimes by a wide margin.
A consistent thread runs through these results: expert decision
rules help when the model also has the prior, and not before. The
Expert + prior combination outperforms Expert alone by 0.13–0.22
ROC–AUC across all four models, while Expert alone never beats

Answer to RQ5. Yes. Combining in-context channels improves
performance on the LLM-synthetic suite, with the best configu-
rations clearing the TabPFN-16 baseline by 0.10–0.14 ROC–AUC
depending on the model. The dominant combinations are Expert +
prior (best for Qwen3-1.7B at 0.732 and Qwen3-14B at 0.862) and
the full triple Few-shot + expert + prior (best for Qwen3-8B at 0.829
and GPT-4o-mini at 0.818). Two qualifications matter. First, expert
rules require the contextual grounding of the prior to be useful –
Expert alone never wins. Second, the smallest model (Qwen3-1.7B)
cannot exploit its own distilled rules on top of few-shot examples,
indicating a minimum capacity threshold for rule-following.
5.6
RQ6: How does dataset complexity influence
LLM performance on tabular tasks?
The previous section showed that LLMs use prior knowledge and
few-shot examples particularly well on LLM-synthetic data. A plau-
sible reason is that the LLM-generated datasets carry relational
patterns and rules the model already knows, which makes them
an easier learning target. For practical use we want to be able to
measure this – to quantify dataset complexity, set meaningful dif-
ficulty thresholds, and identify which patterns LLMs can capture
and which lie beyond them.
A number of established methods exist for estimating the intrin-
sic dimensionality of a dataset [4, 10, 11, 14, 15, 24, 28]. To probe
this systematically we built a grid of synthetic datasets with an
MLP generator, varying complexity along three axes: the number
of hidden layers, the number of input features, and the hidden-layer
dimensionality.
Quantifying complexity in synthetic and real datasets. Experi-
ments with TabPFN [20] confirm that the proposed grid is meaning-
ful. TabPFN performance decreases systematically as complexity
rises, and the gain from additional shots flattens past a certain level
(Figure 31 in Appendix D.6), indicating a threshold beyond which
even a state-of-the-art tabular foundation model cannot reliably
learn the underlying patterns. We cross-check the ranking by esti-
mating intrinsic dimensionality of the synthetic datasets through
several methods [10, 14, 15, 24] via the scikit-dimension library
[2], averaging across methods and seeds (Table 15 in Appendix).
The intrinsic-dimension ranking matches the TabPFN-derived one.
TabPFN performance therefore gives a usable coarse-grained proxy
for placing real-world datasets on this complexity scale.
10


## Page 11


Correlation between complexity and performance. We then ran
the LLMs on the same grid (Table 11). The pattern is sharp: every
model picks up a substantial signal on the easiest level and almost
nothing past it. On MLP0 the 0-shot to 16-shot gain is +0.10 for
GPT-4o-mini, +0.09 for Qwen3-1.7B, +0.15 for Qwen3-8B and +0.15
for Qwen3-14B. From MLP1 onward the gains collapse to the noise
level (|Δ| ≤0.05 across all model–level pairs), with both positive
and negative signs and no clear monotone trend (see Table 11).
Statistical aggregation via DeLong’s test combined with Stouffer’s
method (Figure 32 in Appendix D.6, results aggregated across five
generation seeds per level) confirms that the only level at which
adding shots produces a significant gain is MLP0; at MLP1 and be-
yond the few-shot improvement is not statistically distinguishable
from zero.
Model scale matters but does not change the qualitative picture:
the 16-shot result on MLP0 climbs from 0.662 (Qwen3-1.7B) to 0.748
(Qwen3-14B), so larger models extract more from the same easy data.
But on every level above MLP0 all four models hover in the 0.52–
0.60 range regardless of shot count, and the absolute ROC–AUC
drifts steadily downward with complexity. The threshold above
which few-shot stops helping is essentially the same across the
family.
Answer to RQ6. Dataset complexity is a hard ceiling on LLM tabu-
lar performance. On the MLP-synthetic grid every model we test ex-
tracts a clear +0.09 to +0.15 ROC–AUC signal from 16 shots on the
simplest level (MLP0), and essentially nothing past it: at MLP1 and
above the few-shot gain is statistically indistinguishable from zero.
The TabPFN-derived ranking lines up with intrinsic-dimension
estimates from scikit-dimension, so the same ordering can be
used as a coarse-grained difficulty scale for real-world datasets.
Larger models exploit the easy level better in absolute terms but
the complexity threshold itself is roughly model-invariant.
6
Conlcusion
In this paper, we propose TabLLMBench, a benchmark for evalu-
ating LLMs in zero-shot and few-shot in-context learning settings
under data scarcity. The benchmark comprises 95 real-world and
synthetic datasets from 8 domains and enables systematic, sta-
tistically grounded analyses that isolate the effects of (i) tabular
data serialization, (ii) the number of in-context examples, (iii) the
amount of in-context information (task descriptions and column
names) provided in the prompt, and (iv) dataset complexity across
different LLMs. Experimental results demonstrate that LLMs can be
strong zero-shot classifiers for specific domains and low-complexity
datasets. We further show that in-context information must be used
carefully, as combining multiple knowledge sources does not guar-
antee additive gains and can even reduce performance. These find-
ings highlight the need for further research and suggest substantial
practical potential for applying LLMs as tabular classifiers.
GenAI usage disclosure. Generative AI tools were used solely
to polish and refine the wording; no content was generated by these
tools.

A
Datasets
We use four dataset suites: (i) a core real-world suite of 24 binary-
classification tasks spanning eight knowledge domains (Section A.1);
(ii) a new suite of 8 datasets, one per domain, first released after ev-
ery evaluated LLM’s knowledge cutoff (October 2024), used to probe
data leakage and out-of-cutoff generalisation (Section A.2); (iii) a
synthetic suite generated from MLP priors at seven controlled diffi-
culty levels (Section A.3); (iv) an LLM-synthetic suite obtained by
prompting Qwen3-8B or GPT-4o-mini to fabricate tabular records
matching the schemas of the 24 core datasets (Section A.4). Ta-
bles 12, 13 below list per-dataset metadata. Imbalance (% positive)
refers to the prevalence of the positive class in the full dataset.
A.1
Real Datasets (core suite)
The core suite covers eight knowledge domains: healthcare, busi-
ness, people & society, finance, education, software engineering,
crimes & justice, and natural science (three datasets per domain).
Datasets were selected to span small (𝑛= 151, tae) to large (𝑛≈49k,
adult) sample sizes, low (𝑑= 4) to high (𝑑= 41) dimensional-
ity, and both balanced (∼50%) and severely imbalanced (< 10%)
class distributions. Three originally multi-class tasks (tae, steel,
san_francisco_crimes) were collapsed to binary problems by
grouping the two most populous classes against the rest. The full
per-dataset metadata is given in Table 12.
A.2
New Suite (post-cutoff datasets)
To probe whether observed zero-shot performance reflects memo-
risation, we assemble a parallel suite of eight datasets — one per
knowledge domain — first released after the knowledge cutoff of
every LLM evaluated in this paper (October 2024). All datasets
are binary-classification tasks that follow the same preprocessing
pipeline as the core suite.
Subsampling for large datasets. Several datasets in both the core
and new suites contain more than 5,000 instances. To keep the
experimental wall-clock and inference cost tractable for closed-
source and large open models, we subsample every dataset whose
original size exceeds 5,000 down to exactly 1,000 instances. Sub-
sampling is class-stratified (so the positive-class rate reported in
Tables 12 and 13 is preserved) and uses a fixed random seed of 42
for full reproducibility. The Size columns in both tables therefore
report the size used in the experiments, not the source size. Affected
12


## Page 13


datasets are: in the core suite, telco (orig. 7,043 →1,000), adult
(orig. 48,842 →1,000), hiring (orig. 8,995 →1,000), fraud (orig.
14,446 →1,000), san_francisco_crimes (orig. ∼878,049 →1,000),
compas (orig. 5,278 →1,000), and bbbp (orig. 2,039, kept as is). The
new suite contains no dataset exceeding 5,000 instances.
A.3
Synthetic Datasets (MLP-prior)
The synthetic suite is generated by sampling random multi-layer
perceptrons of controlled depth and width, drawing inputs from
N (0, 𝐼), and labelling them by the sign of the network output. Seven
difficulty levels are obtained by varying layer count, hidden size,
and input dimension; the resulting datasets are used in RQ6 to study
how performance scales with task complexity. Table 14 gives the
configuration of each level, and Table 15 reports the empirically
estimated intrinsic dimensionality, averaged over seeds.
A.4
LLM-synthetic Datasets
For each of the 24 core real-world datasets we generate two LLM-
synthetic counterparts by prompting Qwen3-14B and GPT-4o-mini,
respectively, to fabricate tabular records matching the original
schema and target distribution. The resulting datasets are suffixed
_ls_qwen and _ls_gpt (e.g. diabetes_ls_qwen, diabetes_ls_gpt).
They share the column structure and task description of their real
counterparts but contain no leaked instances from the original data;
this suite is the substrate for the knowledge-source ablations in
RQ2, RQ4, and RQ5.
Generator ↔backbone pairing. The _ls_qwen and _ls_gpt vari-
ants are not interchangeable evaluation targets: each is paired one-
to-one with the model that generated it. The Qwen-generated vari-
ants are evaluated only on the two Qwen ICL backbones (Qwen3-8B
and Qwen3-14B), and the GPT-generated variants only on GPT-4o-
mini. This pairing makes Prompt 3 a clean probe of each model’s
own internal task knowledge: the in-context examples, decision
rules, and inference backbone all come from the same model fam-
ily, so a non-trivial signal can be attributed to that model’s prior
knowledge rather than to a distribution mismatch between the gen-
erator and the evaluator. Cross-evaluation (e.g. running an _ls_gpt
dataset on Qwen) is intentionally omitted throughout the appendix.
Three-step generation procedure. For each (real_dataset, generator_LLM)
pair the synthetic dataset is produced through a chained, three-
turn dialogue.2 The dialogue is structured so that each subsequent
turn receives the full text of the preceding answers as context, en-
suring that the schema introduced in turn 1 is honoured by the
data-generating function in turn 2 and by the labelling rules in
turn 3.
Turn 1 – Schema design. The generator is asked to propose
a prototype dataset from the relevant high-level domain (e.g.
people and society, healthcare) such that the classification
task matches that of the real counterpart. The model decides
on the number of features, their semantics and admissible
ranges/categories.
2All three turns share the same system message: “You are a statistical expert who uses
only clearly measurable data.” This wording is meant to nudge the generator away
from heavily subjective survey-style features.
Turn 2 – Data-generating function. The generator is asked
to return a Python function (using only numpy and pandas)
that materialises rows according to the schema of turn 1.
Numerical features are typically drawn from uniform or
truncated normal distributions over the ranges declared in
turn 1; categorical features are drawn uniformly over their
declared support.
Turn 3 – Decision rules. The generator is asked for three
measurable, threshold-based decision rules that map the
features to a binary target label. These three rules form the
ground-truth oracle used to label rows produced in turn 2: a
row is labelled positive if at least one of the three rules fires,
and negative otherwise (the labelling logic visible inside the
turn-3 code is what is actually executed).
The exact turn-1, turn-2 and turn-3 prompt templates, together
with one fully worked example (the diabetes_ls_qwen dialogue),
are reproduced in Section B.4.
Generation hyperparameters. Both generators are queried with
their default decoding configuration (temperature, top-𝑝, and max_new_tokens
left at the provider’s defaults; no custom sampling overrides). We do
not tune any decoding hyperparameters per dataset or per turn. For
each (real_dataset, generator_LLM) pair we retain all rows
produced in turn 2 as the final synthetic dataset (no rejection sam-
pling, no rebalancing). The resulting class imbalance is therefore
the natural outcome of the turn-3 oracle applied to the turn-2 dis-
tribution.

## Page 17


B
Prompts
We evaluate every model under three prompt regimes, each isolat-
ing a different combination of knowledge sources. All three regimes
share a common system message that instructs the model to emit a
single token, 0 or 1, with no additional text. The variable component
across regimes is a per-dataset task description (which embeds the
target semantics) and an optional decision-rule block (which embeds
external expert knowledge synthesised by an auxiliary LLM).
Below we give the template for each regime followed by one
fully instantiated example. Full per-dataset task descriptions and
decision rules used in the experiments are released with the paper’s
code repository.
B.1
Prompt 1 — Contextual (full task
description, no examples)
Template.
The contextual prompt describes the task, explains
what the input features represent, and defines the meaning of both
target labels. The features are then serialised one of five ways (see
Section 3.1).
For the given input features 〈of [entity]〉you need to predict
〈[task in natural language]〉. Your output should be just a single
number representing a binary class prediction: 0 (〈[negative-
class meaning]〉) or 1 (〈[positive-class meaning]〉). Do not
predict any other tokens, only 0 or 1.
Example (diabetes).
For the given input features you need to predict, based on
diagnostic measurements, whether a patient has diabetes.
Your output should be just a single number representing a
binary class prediction: 0 (does not have diabetes) or 1 (has
diabetes). Do not predict any other tokens, only 0 or 1.
B.2
Prompt 2 — Context-free (query only)
Template.
The context-free prompt strips every domain hint
from the task. The model sees only the serialised features and a
generic two-class instruction; no entity, no target semantics, no
column meaning. This is the prompt used to isolate the contribution
of in-context demonstrations from prior knowledge.
For the given input features you need to classify rows into two
classes: 0 or 1. Do not predict any other tokens, only 0 or 1.
Example.
Identical for every dataset — the verbatim template
above.
B.3
Prompt 3 — Knowledge-augmented (task
description + decision rules)
Template.
The knowledge-augmented prompt extends Prompt 1
with an additional block listing the decision rules by which the
classes were generated. These rules are produced by an auxiliary LLM
(Qwen3-8B for the _ls_qwen suite and DeepSeek for the _ls_gpt
suite) as a synthetic-data annotation step; they are not ground-truth
rules for the real datasets and serve as a structured prior of external
expert knowledge.
〈Prompt 1 task description〉
Here are the decision rules by
which the classes were generated: 〈[rule block — typically 3
rules expressed as threshold conditions or short Python func-
tions]〉
Example (diabetes, decision rules synthesised by Qwen3-
8B).
For the given input features you need to predict, based on
diagnostic measurements, whether a patient has diabetes.
Your output should be just a single number representing a
binary class prediction: 0 (does not have diabetes) or 1 (has
diabetes). Do not predict any other tokens, only 0 or 1. Here
are the decision rules by which the classes were generated:
Rule 1 (High Glucose + High BMI →Diabetes Risk). If
Glucose > 120 AND BMI > 30, then Diabetes = 1.
Rule 2 (Age + Family History →Increased Risk). If Age
> 50 AND FamilyHistory == 1, then Diabetes = 1.
Rule 3 (High Insulin + Low Physical Activity →Dia-
betes Risk). If Insulin > 150 AND PhysicalActivity <
100, then Diabetes = 1.
Coverage of Prompt 3. Prompt 3 is applied only to the LLM-
synthetic suite (the _ls_qwen and _ls_gpt variants of the 24 core
datasets), because the decision rules are valid only for data gen-
erated under those same rules; applying them to the real datasets
would introduce label-rule mismatch. This explains why Prompt 3
columns are empty for real and new-suite datasets throughout the
per-domain tables in Appendix E.
Per-dataset prompt text. Full prompt texts for all 24 core datasets,
all 8 new-suite datasets, and all decision-rule blocks (one per dataset
× rule source) are included verbatim in the code repository accompa-
nying the paper, under prompts/prompt1.json, prompts/prompt2.json,
and prompts/prompt3_〈qwen|gpt〉.json.
B.4
Prompts used to generate the
LLM-synthetic suite
The LLM-synthetic suite described in Section A.4 is produced through
a three-turn chained dialogue. All three turns share the same system
message:
You are a statistical expert who uses only clearly mea-
surable data.
The user messages are templated on the real dataset’s domain 𝐷
(e.g. healthcare, people and society) and the real-task description 𝑇
(e.g. predict based on diagnostic measurements whether a patient has
diabetes). The literal strings are as follows.
Turn 1 — Schema design.
Generate a prototype dataset from the 𝐷domain. Use any
number of features, the classification task should be to 𝑇.
Turn 2 — Data-generating function. The verbatim text of the
answer to turn 1 (denoted 𝐴1) is appended to the user message
before turn 2 is sent:
Generate python functions to create a dataset with the same
features following the given prototype.
𝐴1
17


## Page 18


Turn 3 — Decision rules. The verbatim text of 𝐴2 is appended to
the user message before turn 3 is sent:
Generate python functions with 3 decision rules for determin-
ing a target label based on the features of this 𝐷dataset.
𝐴2
Materialisation. After turn 3 we extract the Python code blocks
from 𝐴2 (data-generating function) and 𝐴3 (three decision rules),
execute them in a sandboxed interpreter, and assemble the final
synthetic table. A row is labelled positive iff at least one of the three
turn-3 rules fires and negative otherwise.
Worked example (diabetes, Qwen3-14B).. The full transcripts of𝐴1,
𝐴2 and 𝐴3 for the diabetes_ls_qwen dialogue (used as the running
illustration in Section B.4) are reproduced verbatim in the supple-
mentary material accompanying the paper. Briefly: turn 1 proposed
an 11-feature schema covering standard diabetic-risk markers (Age,
BMI, Glucose, Insulin, etc.); turn 2 returned a NumPy/Pandas func-
tion that draws each feature from a clipped normal or uniform dis-
tribution; turn 3 emitted three threshold rules — (i) Glucose > 120
AND BMI
>
30, (ii) Age
>
50 AND FamilyHistory == 1,
(iii) Insulin > 150 AND PhysicalActivity < 100 — which
are OR-combined into the final binary label.
18


## Page 19


C
Statistical Methodology
C.1
Simulation-based test against the random
baseline
We construct an adaptive random baseline by sampling class prob-
ability vectors from a symmetric Dirichlet distribution with pa-
rameters 𝛼= [1, . . . , 1]. For each target dataset, this procedure is
repeated 𝑁= 1000 times, yielding an empirical null distribution of
ROC AUC scores that characterizes expected performance under
random guessing given the dataset structure. For each experimental
configuration (model, inference mode, and dataset), we conduct a
one-sample t-test with the null hypothesis that the observed ROC
AUC is equal to the mean of the corresponding null distribution.
C.2
DeLong’s test and Stouffer’s method of
𝑝-value aggregation
For each dataset and each specific experimental condition (defined
by Model, Mode, and a specific𝑘value), the statistical significance of
the difference between the 𝑘-shot ROC-AUC and the corresponding
zero-shot ROC-AUC is assessed. This is performed using DeLong’s
test for two correlated ROC curves [8]. This non-parametric test
is specifically designed to compare AUCs derived from the same
set of test instances, making it ideal for this paired, within-dataset
comparison. The 𝑝-values obtained from DeLong’s test across all
datasets for a given experimental condition are not independent
estimates but are observations of the same underlying 𝑘-shot effect
across different tasks. To obtain a global assessment of significance
for each condition, these 𝑝-values are aggregated using Stouffer’s
method.
C.3
Fisher’s Exact Method (FEM) for
aggregating 𝑡-test 𝑝-values
In Section C.1 we describe how, for each combination of model,
inference mode, and dataset, we obtain a one-sample 𝑡-test 𝑝-value
comparing the observed ROC–AUC to the Dirichlet random base-
line. To obtain a single global verdict on whether a given (model,
mode) combination beats chance across datasets, we combine the
per-dataset 𝑝-values using Fisher’s exact method [12, 13], also re-
ferred to in the meta-analysis literature as the fixed-effect 𝑝-value
aggregation (FEM). Given𝑘per-dataset 𝑝-values 𝑝1, . . . , 𝑝𝑘obtained
under independent null hypotheses, Fisher’s combined statistic is
𝑋2
2𝑘= −2
𝑘
∑︁
𝑖=1
ln𝑝𝑖,
(1)
which, under the joint null that every individual null holds, follows a
𝜒2 distribution with 2𝑘degrees of freedom. The aggregated 𝑝-value
is then Pr 𝜒2
2𝑘≥𝑋2
2𝑘
. We use this aggregation whenever the main
text reports a single “FEM aggregated 𝑝-value” across datasets (e.g.
in Section 4.1). When the aggregated 𝑝-value is below the numerical
underflow threshold of double-precision floating-point arithmetic
(∼10−308) it is reported as “𝑝≈0”; this regime is hit whenever
even one term −2 ln𝑝𝑖becomes large enough to dominate the sum
(e.g. several datasets yielding 𝑝𝑖< 10−40 each). FEM is preferred
over Stouffer’s method (Section C.2) for this particular aggregation
because it is one-sided by construction and has higher power against
very small 𝑝𝑖.

## Page 20


D
Visual results per research question
This appendix collects the principal figures supporting the research-
question analyses of the main text, one block per RQ 1–6. Each
block reproduces the headline plot(s) on which the corresponding
conclusions rely; the underlying numbers and statistical tests are
reported in the main text and in the per-domain tables of Section E.
D.1
RQ 1 — Zero-shot competitiveness against
TabPFN
RQ 1 asks whether LLMs are competitive in zero-shot tabular clas-
sification. We compare the four LLMs against TabPFN (16-shot)
across the eight real-world domains of the core suite (Figures 7, 8).
For each backbone we report aggregated ROC–AUC by domain and
across the classic vs. post-cutoff (“new”) subsets.
Figure 7: Per-domain mean ROC–AUC at 0-shot for the four
LLMs vs. 16-shot TabPFN (dashed). Each axis is one domain.
Mid-to-large LLMs cluster near TabPFN-16 across most do-
mains and exceed it on healthcare.
D.2
RQ 2 — In-context information vs. prior
knowledge
RQ 2 disentangles the contributions of pretrained prior knowledge
and in-context information by progressively removing contextual
cues from the prompt. Figure 10 shows zero-shot ROC–AUC under
the four Graded Context Configurations (see Table 3 in the protocol).
Each bar group is one model; within each group, the four bars
correspond to the four configurations from Cols-Context (most
informative) to NoCols-NoContext (least informative). The dashed
red line indicates the Δ between the two endpoints.
D.3
RQ 3 — Few-shot effectiveness
RQ 3 quantifies the gains from few-shot demonstrations across
𝑘∈{0, 4, 8, 16, 32, 64} (Figures 11–14).
Figure 8: Per-domain comparison: GPT-4o-mini at 0-shot,
GPT-4o-mini at 16-shot, and TabPFN at 16-shot. The 0-shot
LLM is already within range of 16-shot TabPFN on most
domains.
Figure 9: LLMs vs. TabPFN as a function of shot count, aver-
aged over the real-world suite. Solid lines show each LLM;
dashed lines show TabPFN; the horizontal reference line at
0.5 is the random baseline.
D.4
RQ 4 — Context contribution across shot
regimes
RQ 4 extends RQ 2 to the few-shot regime: as the shot count 𝑘
grows, how much of the headline performance is still attributable
to the contextual cues in the prompt? Figures 15–19 repeat the
four-way context comparison of RQ 2 at 𝑘∈{4, 8, 16, 32, 64}. Read
together with Figure 10, they show that the gap between full-context
and minimal-context configurations shrinks substantially with 𝑘,
indicating partial substitution between in-context information and
few-shot demonstrations.

Figure 10: Prior-knowledge gradual increment on real

NoContext quantifies the contribution of in-context infor-

above each group give the per-model effect size.
Figure 11: ROC–AUC vs. number of in-context shots on the
real-world suite (Cols-Context configuration). Mid-to-large
LLMs improve monotonically with 𝑘but saturate near 𝑘= 16.
Figure 12: Same plot as Figure 11 extended to the full shot

Figure 13: 0-shot ROC–AUC on classic (pre-cutoff) vs. new

exhibits a moderate drop across all LLMs, comparable to

Figure 14: Same comparison as Figure 13 but at 16 shots. Few-

Figure 15: Prior-knowledge gradual increment on real
datasets at 4-shot.

## Page 25


E
Per-domain raw results
This appendix collects the per-domain, per-dataset ROC–AUC ta-
bles that underlie the aggregated comparisons in the main paper
and in Sections D.1–D.6. Each table fixes a single prompt regime
and breaks results down by (dataset, serialization, model, shot-count).
Within each table, rows are grouped by dataset and split into one
row per serialization; columns are grouped first by model (Qwen3-
8B, Qwen3-14B, GPT-4o-mini, TabPFN) and then by shot-count
(0/4/8/16/32/64). Entries are means ± standard deviations of test
ROC–AUC across seeds. Dashes (“–”) indicate configurations that
were not run; in particular, TabPFN consumes the raw feature ma-
trix directly and is therefore reported only once per dataset, with
no serialization or shot-count axis.
The nine domains follow the taxonomy introduced in Section A:
business, education, finance, healthcare, law, natural_science, peo-
ple_society, software_engineering, and the controlled synthetic suite
(MLP-prior datasets, Section A.3). The synthetic table differs slightly
in row content (datasets are identified by their MLP-prior generator
parameters rather than by name) but uses the same column layout.
E.1
Prompt 1 — full task description
We start with the simplest contextual prompt (Prompt 1, Section B.1):
the model is shown the dataset’s task description and the test in-
stance, with no decision rules. This is the canonical “contextual ICL”
setting and is used throughout the main paper as the default con-
textual baseline. For each of the eight real domains we report two
complementary cross-sections: one indexed by serialization format
(Section E.1.1) and one indexed by shot count (Section E.1.2). The
first cross-section is the natural view for studying how the textual
rendering of a row interacts with the LLM’s representation; the sec-
ond is the natural view for studying sample-efficiency of in-context
learning and for comparing LLM ICL against the strong classical
baselines (TabPFN, logistic regression, random forest, XGBoost) on
the same row-budget axis. The MLP-prior synthetic domain is not
evaluated under Prompt 1 (its placeholder feature names carry no
semantics); its tables appear under Prompt 2 in Section E.2.
E.1.1
By serialization format. The tables in this section fix Prompt
1 and break results down by (dataset, serialization, model, shot-
count): rows are grouped by dataset and split into one row per
serialization, while columns are grouped first by model and then by
shot-count (0/4/8/16/32/64). TabPFN does not consume text serializa-
tions and is therefore omitted from these views (it appears instead
in the by shot-count tables of Section E.1.2).
E.1.2
By shot count. The tables in this section also fix Prompt
1, but break results down by (dataset, shot-count, model, inference
mode): rows are grouped by dataset and split into one row per shot-
count (0/4/8/16/32/64); columns are grouped by model, and for Qwen
models further split into two sub-columns gen/fwd (autoregressive
generation versus single forward pass with masked label-token
logits). GPT-4o-mini is API-only and reports a single gen column.
The right-hand block of each table reports the classical-ML baselines
(TabPFN, logistic regression, random forest, XGBoost) on the same
shot-count axis; these have no inference-mode distinction and so
occupy single columns. All cells in this section use the feat_val
serialization (i.e. the canonical “feature: value, feature: value, ...”
rendering); the other serializations appear in Section E.1.1.

Table 31: Per-domain results, software_engineering suite, Prompt 1 (full task description, no decision rules). Cells show ROC–
AUC, mean±std across seeds, broken down by shot-count (rows) and model × inference mode (columns). For Qwen models
the two sub-columns are gen (autoregressive) and fwd (single forward pass with masked label-token logits). GPT-4o-mini is
API-only and supports gen only. Dashes indicate configurations not run.
41


## Page 42


E.2
Prompt 2 — context-free (query only)
We turn to the strictest contextless setting (Prompt 2, Section B.2):
the model receives the test row and (in the 𝑘-shot variants) 𝑘la-
belled in-context examples, but no task description and no deci-
sion rules. This isolates how much the LLM can extract purely
from pattern-matching on the in-context examples themselves,
with no help from prior natural-language knowledge of the task.
We again give the two complementary cross-sections defined in
Section E.1: indexed by serialization (Section E.2.1) and by shot
count (Section E.2.2). The MLP-prior synthetic suite appears only
in this section: its feature names are anonymous placeholders
(f0, f1, . . . , h0, h1, . . . ) and carry no human-interpretable
semantics, so the context-bearing Prompts 1 and 3 are inapplicable
and were not run on it.
E.2.1
By serialization format. The tables follow the same layout
as Section E.1.1: rows are grouped by dataset and split into one
row per serialization, columns are grouped by model and then by
shot-count. TabPFN is again omitted because it does not consume
text serializations; it appears with its raw-feature interface in the
by shot-count tables (Section E.2.2).
E.2.2
By shot count. The layout matches Section E.1.2: rows are
indexed by shot-count (0/4/8/16/32/64); columns report each model
under both inference modes (gen/fwd) where applicable, followed
by the classical-ML baselines. All cells use the feat_val serial-
ization. The MLP-prior synthetic suite appears at the bottom (see
Section E.2.1).

across seeds, broken down by shot-count (rows) and model × inference mode (columns). For Qwen models the two sub-columns

57


## Page 58


E.3
Prompt 3 — knowledge-augmented (task
description + decision rules)
The third prompt regime (Prompt 3, Section B.3) is the most in-
formative: in addition to the dataset’s task description it includes
a block of natural-language decision rules, distilled from an auxil-
iary LLM through the same three-turn pipeline used to produce
the LLM-synthetic suite (Section A.4). Because each decision-rule
block is generated jointly with, and paired one-to-one to, a specific
LLM-synthetic dataset, Prompt 3 is evaluated only on the LLM-
synthetic suite – the datasets with the suffixes _ls_qwen (Qwen3-
14B-distilled rules and rows) and _ls_gpt (GPT-4o-mini-distilled
rules and rows). The real-data counterparts (e.g. diabetes, telco)
and the MLP-prior synthetic suite do not appear in this section.
Generator-backbone pairing. To measure Prompt 3 as a probe
of each model’s own internal task knowledge, we tie each LLM-
synthetic dataset to a single inference backbone. The _ls_qwen
variants – whose rows and decision rules were both produced
by Qwen3-14B – are run only on the two Qwen ICL backbones
(Qwen3-8B and Qwen3-14B). The _ls_gpt variants – generated
by GPT-4o-mini – are run only on GPT-4o-mini. Cross-evaluation
(e.g. running an _ls_gpt dataset on Qwen) is intentionally not
reported: it would mix a Qwen-internal prior with a GPT-shaped
data distribution and rule set, making the resulting score impossible
to attribute to either factor. The empty (–) blocks in the tables below
reflect this design choice, not missing runs.
The two cross-sections, by serialization and by shot count, match
those of Sections E.1 and E.2.
E.3.1
By serialization format. The layout follows Sections E.1.1
and E.2.1: rows are grouped by dataset and split into one row per
serialization; columns are grouped first by model and then by shot-
count. TabPFN is omitted (it does not consume text serializations)
and reappears with its raw-feature interface in the by shot-count
tables (Section E.3.2).

Table 55: Per-domain results, natural_science suite, Prompt 3 (knowledge-augmented: task description plus auxiliary LLM-
elicited decision rules). Each LLM-synthetic dataset is paired one-to-one with its generator: _ls_qwen variants are generated by
Qwen3-14B and evaluated only on the two Qwen ICL backbones, while _ls_gpt variants are generated by GPT-4o-mini and
evaluated only on GPT-4o-mini. This pairing isolates each model’s reliance on its own internal knowledge of the (distilled)
task. Cells show ROC–AUC, mean±std across seeds, broken down by serialization (rows within each dataset) and number of
in-context shots (columns within each model block). TabPFN is omitted because it does not consume text serializations (its
raw-feature performance is reported in the by shot-count tables, Section E.3.2). Dashes indicate configurations not run.

Table 57: Per-domain results, software_engineering suite, Prompt 3 (knowledge-augmented: task description plus auxiliary
LLM-elicited decision rules). Each LLM-synthetic dataset is paired one-to-one with its generator: _ls_qwen variants are generated
by Qwen3-14B and evaluated only on the two Qwen ICL backbones, while _ls_gpt variants are generated by GPT-4o-mini and
evaluated only on GPT-4o-mini. This pairing isolates each model’s reliance on its own internal knowledge of the (distilled)
task. Cells show ROC–AUC, mean±std across seeds, broken down by serialization (rows within each dataset) and number of
in-context shots (columns within each model block). TabPFN is omitted because it does not consume text serializations (its
raw-feature performance is reported in the by shot-count tables, Section E.3.2). Dashes indicate configurations not run.
62


## Page 63


E.3.2
By shot count. The layout matches Section E.1.2: rows are
indexed by shot-count (0/4/8/16/32/64); columns report each model
under both inference modes (gen/fwd) where applicable, followed
by the classical-ML baselines. All cells use the feat_val serial-
ization. The generator-backbone pairing described above means
the Qwen columns are populated only for _ls_qwen rows, and the
GPT-4o-mini column only for _ls_gpt rows; the classical baselines
are run on both variants in all cases.

Table 60: Per-domain results, finance suite, Prompt 3 (knowledge-augmented: task description plus auxiliary LLM-elicited
decision rules), feat_val serialization. Each LLM-synthetic dataset is paired one-to-one with its generator: _ls_qwen variants
are generated by Qwen3-14B and evaluated only on the two Qwen ICL backbones, while _ls_gpt variants are generated by
GPT-4o-mini and evaluated only on GPT-4o-mini. This pairing isolates each model’s reliance on its own internal knowledge of
the (distilled) task. Cells show ROC–AUC, mean±std across seeds, broken down by shot-count (rows) and model × inference
mode (columns). For Qwen models the two sub-columns are gen (autoregressive) and fwd (single forward pass with masked
label-token logits); GPT-4o-mini is API-only and reports a single gen column. Dashes indicate configurations not run.

Table 63: Per-domain results, natural_science suite, Prompt 3 (knowledge-augmented: task description plus auxiliary LLM-
elicited decision rules), feat_val serialization. Each LLM-synthetic dataset is paired one-to-one with its generator: _ls_qwen
variants are generated by Qwen3-14B and evaluated only on the two Qwen ICL backbones, while _ls_gpt variants are generated
by GPT-4o-mini and evaluated only on GPT-4o-mini. This pairing isolates each model’s reliance on its own internal knowledge
of the (distilled) task. Cells show ROC–AUC, mean±std across seeds, broken down by shot-count (rows) and model × inference
mode (columns). For Qwen models the two sub-columns are gen (autoregressive) and fwd (single forward pass with masked
label-token logits); GPT-4o-mini is API-only and reports a single gen column. Dashes indicate configurations not run.
